linen Cluny laces remain. They are fashionable laces. You have already bought more than ten 
times the amount we have left. But ‘‘good’’ as the laces are, they were bought for summer, 
and must sell in their season. Prices, therefore, drop from 35c, 39c, 50c and 59e, to 19¢. 
These are real linen laces in the charming Cluny patterns. (See window display.) 
Since every thread is purest linen, the laces will wash and wear indefinitely. 
——Hand loom work, hence thegdesigns are intricate, interesting ‘and artistic. 
Matched sets of edges and insertings, in widths from 4 to 7 inches. 
——ldeal for trimming bed spreads, table covers and household linens.

‘MEET SOUTHERN BANKERS 


Discuss Proposed $50,000,- | 
000 Distribution 


(By Associated Press.) | 
j. WASHINGTON, Aug. 6.—Secretary 
|McAdoo's invitation to southern bank- 
ers to attend a conference at the treas- 
‘ry department tomorrow to discuss 
\the proposed distribution of $50,000,000 
of government funds to help move, 
crops, mas met with almost unanimous 
response. Only a few instances have 
jthe banks or clearing house associa- 
‘tions notified the department that ‘no 
representatives would be sent and then 
it was stated they believed their cities 
would be fairly treated in the proposed | 
distribution. } 

Preparations for the reception of the 
bankers have been completed. It is 
proposed now td hold the conference in 
the office of Assistant Secretary Wil-| 
liams. who with Secretary McAdoo and | 
R. B. Daskam, chief of the division of | 
public moneys, will attend the confer- | 
ence. Assistant Secretary Williams) 
said that so far seventy-five bankers | 
fiom scuthern cities had accepted the | 
secretary's invitation and that his of- 
fices might be too small for the confer- 
ence. 

Protests from a number of cities in 
the south, west and far west have been 
been received by letter and telegram be- 
case they would not be included in the 
proj osed. distribution of funds, It was 
indicated at the department that the 
list would not be increased, however, 
although Lexington, Ky.; Duluth#Minn., 
and Fort-Worth, Tex., were added to- 
day. Jt was explained they were in- 
cluded in the original list, but were 
omitted when it was made public.

The City’s Vital Need 
Of Amp!te Water Supply. 


Atlanta has just emerged from a crisis that warns 
Council of the imperative need of ampler facilities 
for the public water supply. Some thirty days ago 
the main pump at the river station became disabled 
and, as a matter of necessity, was shut down for 
repairs. During all that time the city’s welfare so 
far as water is concerned was dependent on machin- 
ery which, had it suffered the slightest accident, 
would ha left t community with less than half 


the volume of water consumed each day under nor- 


mal conditions. 

The perils of such a situation are really fearful 
to contemplate. Aside from the discomforts to the 
individual home and the impediments to industry 
and business, so serious a shortage in the water 
supply might become a menace to property and 
health and to life itself. What if fire should break 


\ 
out when the main pump was not operating and the 


others, too, were unayailable! The mere possibility 
or thought of such an occurrence should stir Council 
to the promptest action possible. 

the 


the 


Manager Smith, of waterworks department, 


has repeatedly urved need of more adequate 
safeguards against 
He de 
million 


Dithlic’s security 


emergencies which might arise. 


ares that an additional capacity of twenty 


gallons at each station is essential to the 


This would call for an estimated 


expenditure of between two hundrec and fifty and 
three hundred thousand dollars. But whatever the 
cost may be it is not to be weighed against the 
tragic risk to which continued neglect of this matter 
will expose the people's well-being. 


Without a sufficient water supply, proof against 


all e 


rgencies, the ¢ity’s most vital interests are at 


stake. Sanitation, property and life should no longer 
be jeopardized.

Spare a Moment’s Time 
For This Important Bill. 


All right-thinking people will rejcice in the pros- 
pect that the laws of Georgia are soon to be purged 


of that monstrous statute which denies a mother 
any legal right to the stody of her child. The bill 
to this end which was recently introduced by Repre- 


sentatives Smith, c* Fulton, and Olive, of Richmond, 


has been favora>ly reported after a careful hearing 


by the General Judic committee of the House. 


is appar- 
parently so united in support of this measure that 


ary 


Legislative as well as popular sentient 
its enactment is virtually assured, if it is given its 


due place on the calendar. 


Indeed, there seems to be no difference of opinion 
as to the wisdom and justice of recognizing by law 
the rights of a mother to her children. Prominent 
superior court judges in different parts of the State 
have urged legislation of this character. The rank 
and file of representative attorneys are equally earn- 
est in their indorsement 


of such a measure; and as 
for the attitude of the public generally, it is one of 


eager insistence that the present inhuman law be 
changed 

In these circumstances, the Legislature should 
talk particular pains to dispose of this important 
issue before the end of its current session. The 
pending bill is in every respect a well-considered 
one. It has the committee's approval, based upon a 
thorough examination. Its passage will consume ap- 
preciably no time either in the House or the Senate, 
for, it is not the Kind of measure that will provoke 
debate. It will not stand in the way of any other 
business or delay the progress of any other bill. 

Surely, then, the Legislature will spare a few 
minutes of its time to relieve this State of the 
shame from its present cruel law 
against of motherl.ood.

This Would Make 
Atlanta World Famous. 
If it be true that the growth of a single blade of 
grass is as wonderful as the history of any empire 
that ever rose or fell, how absorbingly interesting 
would it be to follow stage by stage the development 
and the wide human uses of a plant like cotton, to 
trace its unfolding power from seed to snowy harvest, 
to watch its myriad transformations into raiment 
and food and its thousand journeys through the 
trade and life of the world! Men have traveled to 
distant lands to see things much less marvelous and 
have pored over shelffuls of books with far less profit. 
One of the most engaging enterprises ever con- 
ceived is that of establishing a complete and perma- 
nent exhibition of cotton and cotton products in the 
new Chamber of Commerce building, where it will 
be open to visitors the year around and will prove 
a distinctive advertisement not only for Atlanta but 
This plan has suggested itself 
in connection with the permanent display of Atlanta- 


made goods that is to be established in the near 
future. 


for the entire South. 


It is prgposed to devote an entire floor of the 
Chamber of Commerce building to the cotton in- 
dustry in its manifold phases. One section of the 
exhibit will show how cotton is grown. Another will 
se. forth its manufacture; and so in turn each prod- 
uct and by-product of cotton will be illustrated in 
their amazing variety of uses. Cotton hulls alone 
are more broadly valuable today than was the lint 
itself Cotton seed, with their 
rich oils for commercial and table supplies, are the 
foundation of a vast and comparatively new field of 
industry. This plant is, indeed, more wonderful 
than any which ever grew in a wizard’s garden. 

An exhibit demonstrating all these uses and pro- 
cesses would become one of the great show-places of 
the South. It would be the only attraction of its 
kind in the world. To Southern people themselves, 
it would be a revelation; and to visitors from other 
sections, it would appeal more keenly than any other 
form of entertainment or education they could be 
offered. 


twenty years ago. 


The members of the Georgia Cotton Seed Crush- 
ers’ Association anc kindred interests are supporting 
this project with such hearty enthusiasm that its 
success is virtually assured. Such funds as are .e- 
cessary to carry the enterprise to completion will be 
an investment rather than a donation, an investment 
for the progress of Atlanta and the South, and for 


all the interests that are directly or indirectly con- 
cerned.

It is difficult for any man to overlook the woman 
who has all the hair she wants piled on her head. 


The Mexican Macbeth. 


Huerta of Mexico is a miniature Macbeth. 

In his last tottering hours, he swaggers most de- 
fiantly and wears his gaudiest trappings. 

Despised by the rank and file of his countrymen 
for his treacherous and uncivilized course toward a 
rightfully elected President, forsaken by most of the 
army over which he once held sway and by many 
of his personal followers, he is hemmed in the cap- 
ital with little or no authority except over imme- 
diately adjacent districts. 

Yet, this is the Huerta who scorns the good offices 
of the United States and declares that he will not 
recognize or treat with Ex-Governor Lind whom 
President Wilson expects to send to Mexico as the 
administration’s special representative. 

If the present drift of affairs continues a little 
longer, Huerta will treat with no one simply for the 
reason that he will be fairly on the path to the 
Everlasting Bonfire. 

If he is determined to press his rash policy to a 
finish, he will soon be paying the penalty of that 
“vaulting ambition which o’erleaps itself.” 

Sober Mexican citizens have hoped to effect an 
amicable understarding among the factions now at 
war, their plan being the voluntary retirement of 
Huerta in favor or some non-partisan statesman who 
would serve as provisional president until a regular 
election could be held. 

But the would-be dictator will hear to no such 
peaceful project. 

“Our sovereignty,” he says with a royal emphasis 
of the word, must be maintained at all hazards. The 
doughty general evidently 
boots on 

In so far as the interests.and duties of the United 
States in this matter are concerned, they are being 
ably directed by the administration.

OUR OWN TRAVELOGUES. 

The French are a gay people, fond of light wines 
and dancing, and Paris is a beautiful city full of open 
air cafes (pronouncéd caffays). Even the littlest chil- 
dren here speak French, which is considered remarka- 
ble by tourists, Cook's especially. I speak French, too, 
but the waiter usually answers in English. This city 
is unpopular with smokers, because the matches (les 
alumnettes) will rarely light. 

HENRY SYDNOR HARRISON. 

Paris, Fr. 


Supplementing which, and proving our contention 
that the w. k. novelist should have stuck to versifying, 
comes: 
Dear F. P. At’ Why grind yon grind 
When you might come take Paris on. 
Cafes before, Musees behind— 
You'd love the place. 
H. HARRISON. 


THE DIARY OF OUR OWN SAMUEL PEPYS. 

August 1—To my dentist’s and he pained me but 
little and thence with Mistress Alma to luncheon and 
thence to my office at some writing. Home upon an 
omnibus and it came on to rain and a lady a lighting 
with me 1 did hold my umbrella over her to save her 
white hat and so to the door of her house, but I did 
say nought to her, lest she deem me overbold and she 
nought to me, thinking me, I believe, an impudent 
churl, albeit I did but wish to keep her hat dry. Read 
this night Floyd Dell's “Women as World Builders,” 
a fine book, too, and interesting as profound. 

2.—Up and all morning at my scrivening and then 
came A, Guiterman the poet and Mistress Vida and 
took us to Larchmont by oll-waggon, where we did 
play at tennis with M. Appelbaum and thence all to 
dinner and home, but later than I liked, 

8—Up by six and hard at work until nine when 
came J. Leopold the chirurgeon and took us in his new 
petrol-car to Mistress Heloise’s where I did play at 
tennis all the day and with fair success, With F. 
Wenck the ferryman to see his boat, very good, too, 
and Fred very happy and a fine brave soul he is, mau- 
gre the fiery spirit he had when he was a pamphieteer. 
In the evening all on A. Hays’s boat the Ora II. and a 
merry party and I did have many sandwiches of ham 
and of chicken and a salad of fish and many beakers 
of grape-juice and ginger beer and sarsaparilla and 
young Mr. Wurms and A. Hays and my wife and Mis- 
tress Margaret Mason, a fair girl from St. Botolphe’s 
town, did leap into the sound, but that I deemed too 
great exertion, so I did stay and amuse myself with 
some cakes Mistress Heloise had baked with the quiet 
skill she hath in all things.

PLAISIZ, D'AMOUR. 
FROM THE FRENCH OF DE FLORIAN. 
Delight of Love! for but a moment born; 
- Despair of Love; With life its end shall be. 
All lost for her, ingratitude I see; 
New love she welcomes, leaving me in scorn. 


Delight of Love! for but a moment born; 
Despair of Love! With life its end shall be, 
While flows that stream through night until the morn, 
And sepulchres its murmur in the sta. 


“Forever thine,” her promise came to me; 
Still flows the stream, but she is now forsworn. 
Delight of Love! for but a moment born; 
Despair of Love! With Mfe its end shall be.

Alfred has discovered the Height of Fastidiousness. 
“It exists,” he writes from Newport, “in Motorman 37 
of the Newport and C. R. R.; he wears black goggles 
and carries qa sure-enough feather duster, with which 
he dusts off the controller box and the front platform 
before starting the return trip from Bristol Ferry to 
Newport.”

THESTORY OF SALT 
By Frederic J. Haskin 


In the earliest history of man salt‘ was looked upon 
as one of the luxuries of life. Before Lot's wife was 
turned into a Pillar of salt, this product was recog- 
nized as\a valuable commodity. 
The woman was punished for 
looking backward, but at least 
she was transformed into the 
most coveted substance of that 
period.” Salt has been really the 
forerunner of civilization. | Lit- 
tle of it is used by the savage. 
As primitive man acquired a 
taste for salt and the means 
of gratifying it, his fiercest in- 
stincts vanished and his gentler 
emotions began to dominate him. 
With the early tribes salt was 
so difficult to obtain that the 
saying “He seasons his food with 
salt” was equivalent to saying 
he is rich. A piece of gold might purchase only the 
smallest cake of salt. Salt was so valued that a man 
who had eaten the salt’ of another was honor bound 
to be loyal to him. Consequentely, while a man 
might accept unsalted bread from his enemy, he was 
bound to become his friend if he ate his salt, 
eee 


John MacGregor, the pioneer canoeist, 
tured by an Arab tribe upon the upper Jordan river. 
He was taken with his canoe before the old shaykh 
whose tent was near. As they parleyed, MacGregor 
opened, a pocket hox of fine salt and offered a pinch 
to the shaykh who, having never seen salt so fine and 
white, accepted it thinking it was sugar. As he 
tasted it MacGregor put a pinch into his own mouth 
and laughingly slapped the old man upon the back. 
He was free. Even the other Arabs laughed when 
they understood that ther chief had eaten salt with 
the captive under nis own tent. MacGregor and his 
canoe were eScorted back to the river in triumph. 
The Arab chief was now bound to favor hiny by the 
covenant of salt, the strongest bond of his nation. 

eee 


was cap- 


The increased wealth of the world has developed 
an increased demand for salt, and fortunately for the 
comfort of the human race there seems to be no lack 
in the salt résources of the world. It is to be found 
everywhere, although not always in a form or quantity 
to be readily utilized. The salt production of the 
world is estimated at 15,000,000 tons annually, of which 
the United States produces nearly a third. Next to 
this country Russia is the greatest salt producer of 
the world, although the industry has not been much 
exploited and the methods of securing it are most 
primitive. Russia contains five great salt lakes 
which are believed to comprise the greatest salt dis- 
trict in the world, Their output might be multiplied 
by the use of modern machinery. 

eee 


Great Britain comes after Russia in the value of 
its salt product. The English salt industry is con- 
trolled by a great union which includes practically all 
of the salt opérators, Most English salt is secured 
from natural brine by modern scientific process and its 
quality is recognized throughout the world, Much of 
the German salt comes from native mines owned | 
the government or by members of the nobility. The 
government does not exercise a monopoly in the salt 
industry, but the product manufactured by private 
firms is subject to a taxation. In Italy salt manu- 
fecture is under the direction of the government, with 
the exception of that produced in Sicily. Salt s such 
a luxury in Italy that the poorer people use it spar- 
ingly, even in seasoning their food. In Holland the 
salt is not controlled by the government, and thirty- 
three salt refineries’ produce for domestic use about 
128,000,000 pounds annually. Until recently no foreign 
salt was allowed in Chine. Now a small importation 
is permitted for the use of the foreign residents who 
find the coarse native salt unfit for table use. The 
Japanese government controls the salt industry in that 
country. 

eee 


About 35,000,000 barrels of salt are produced an- 
nually in the United States, the value of which is 
nearly $9,000,000. This includes five grades known 
in trade as table or dairy common fine, common coarse, 
packer’s and solar. Most of this comes from fifteen 
states, New York being recognized as the leading salt 
state with Michigan second. The government record 
of the New York salt product combines it with that of 
Louisiana. The amount for the two states exceeds 
11,000,000 barrels valued at $2,500,000. Michigan’s salt 
yield last year approximated 10,000,000 barrels, so that 
she may head the list of the next salt report. The re- 
cent development of the salt industry in Louisiana has 
already given an estimated yield of 2,000,000 barrels 
which leaves only 9,000,000 for New York, 


The salt resources of Louisiana are now controlled 
by two rival corporations, each of whom insists upon 
preserving a strict secrecy regarding its output. If 
the salt production of the state were given either cor- 
poration by deducting its own production could read- 
ily know the amount produced by the rival firm. It is 
the policy of the government officials who compile re- 
ports to preserve all trade confidences. Consequently, 
while elaborate descriptions are given regarding the 
methods of salt mining prevalent in Louisiana, no fig- 
ures as to its output are obtainable, 

eee 


The New York salt industry is the oldest in this 
country. Syracuse has been a salt center since. 1797. 
Wells in that locality are surik as deep as 300 feet, 
and the brine pumped from them is manufactured 
into salt either by solar or artificial evaporation. In 
Louisiana the salt is mined after the most etficent 
methods known to modern science. The location of 
the first shaft of the larger mine was made in 1898. 
By 1912 a 600-foot level had been reached. Mining 
is carried on there by blasting out masses of rock salt 
on the level with the floor and then cutting down layer 
by dayer. The drills are worked by compressed air 
furnished from a power house outside. The mine is 
lighted by electric light and contains a ventilating 
equipment. The salt is conveyed to the shaft in 
small cars drawn by mules over a narrow gauge steel 
track similar to those used. in northern coal mines. 
The capacity of this mine is said to be 750 tons per 
ten-hour day. 

eee 


Salt is distributed in two forms, in brine such as 
is found in the Syracuse salt wells, and in rocks, such 
as are mined in Louisiana. Sometimes both forms are 
found in the same or in adjoining localities. The 
Michigan industry produces salt from each of these 
two sources. In addition to mining rock salt in Mich- 
igan, artificial brine is made by bringing fresh water 
in contact with it. When the water has become sat- 
urated with salt it is brought to the surface and evap- 
orated. Natural brine also occurs plentifully in some 
parts of Michigan. In Ohio the. natural bring pools 
are sufficient to make it the third salt state of this 
country. 


eee 


The salt resources of Utah ar 
Great Salt Lake is approximately 2,250 square miles 
in area. Excepting the Dead Sea, it is believed ¢ bi 
the densest body of water in the world. = se 
five or six times as salty as sea water. 
obtained by solar evaporation.

Season begins in May when the water from the ieee 
is pumped into great wooden flumes which carr: it 
to the ponds a mile or more away. About 7,000. ° 
8,000 gallons a minute are pumped during a tausioar 
day. There are three sets ofiponds. The settling pond 
is the first into which the water is run, and i. re- 
mains here about ten days. All cinders and foreign 
substances are removed and its salty strength highly 
increased. It is transferred then to the stock pond 
where it remains about ten days more. Here its sa- 
lnity has increased to about 26 per cent, after which 
it is drawn off into the harvest pond.

Southern Christian 
) Citizenship Conference 


The greatest marvel of the last century ts 
precedented development of the material side o 
zation. There has been no correspending mo: 
intellectual growth. Dr, Alfred Russel Walla, 
recent book on “Social Environment and Mor, 
ress,” says: “Our whole system of society 
from top to bottom, and the social environment 
worst the world has ever seen.” 
that he, mentions are adulteration,

bribery, insanttary 
gambling, prostitution, alcoholism, suicide, 


The multiplied and closer relations into which we 
Rave been thrust have created new problems which the 
popular intelligence does not understand, and neq 
obligations which the popular conscience does not an 
cept.” The greatest need of the times—a genenn 
need which includes every desirable reform, moral, go. 
cial, political and industrial, is the promotion of goes 
citizenship and the education of public opinion and of 
the popular conscience. 

We have missed great opportunities in not utitisine 
the public school system of the country to build cha 
acter,#and to aid in making good and honorable men 
and women. A recent writer predicts that some day 
it will be recognized that the state must in self-pro. 
tection undertake the moral training of is ghildren os 
its citizenship will deteriorate below the line of sate. 
ty. It is more important, he says, to make citizens 
than scholars and artisans. 

The Civic League of America stands for good citi. 
senship, the cultivation and expression of healthy pyb. 
Mc sentiment, diffusion of knowledge and promulea. 
tion of truth for righteous civil government, promotion 
of universal prosperity, social service, sound morality 


and the common good. 
. 


The situation demands strenuous and well directed 
action; and with the objects of the league properly 
carried out reform along these lines can be speedily 
implanted on the thought of the nation as the most 
important issue before the American people today. 

Much work has already been done, and it is de 
sired to continue operations through public lectures, 
conferences and conventions; through field secretaries, 
and the distinguished body of men upon whom the 
league will be able to draw for discussion of subjects 
pertinent to this movement; through press bulletin, 
leaflets and pamphlets, and through civic league mem. 
bers, and the co-operation of public-spirited citizens in 
every community. 

For some time past arrangements have neen Boing 
forward for the Southern Christian Citizenship conten 
ence to be held under the auspices of the Civic League 
of America, at the Atlanta auditorium, September 19, 
+. and 21. The program of proceedings, general dis. 
cussion and addresses from speakers of national repus 
tation will be announced in due time, and a Yarge ay. 
tendance is anticipated. 

S. T. NICHOLSON, 
General Secretary Civic League of America, 
Atlanta, Ga, Aug. 4, 1913. 


The Kind of a Woman a 
Man Likes; Robert Hitch- 


ens Gives His Opinion | 


Men like all kinds of women, says Robert Hichens 
in the July Strand Magazine. There are ugly men who 
adore beautiful women, but there are also handsome 
men who worship at the shrines of women who are 
quite unlike Helen of Troy. Many good men haye 
loved, and will love, bad women. Who has not seen 
bad men devoted to saintly women? On the other 
hand, the dwarf is often captivated by the large 
framed woman whose head approaches the ceiling? I 
have known deaf women beloved by talkative men; 
lame women cherished by men who were agile; stupid 
women thought sensible, or even clever, by men who 
were brilliant; affected women solemnly admired by! 
the most natural of men; girls who turned the heads 
of grandfathers, and old women who luréd mere boy 
to their feet. 

Effeminate men often seek “manny’’ women, while 
the delicate woman who never leaves the sofa attracts 
the Nimirod and the hunter of big same. The man 
who does not know “God Save the King” when he 
hears it as often as not marries the woman who is 
“mad about Wagner,” and the man who never goes to 
churches chooses as his helpmate the devout woman 
who visits a “district” and teaches in Sunday school. 
All kinds of women are liked—nay, more, are loved 
by men. Why not? For where is the man who can- 
not find one woman—if not two—to think him what 
he probably thinks himself, the most perfect man in 
the world—until the honeymoon is waning? 


Measuring a Plant's Drink 


When we cut flowers we put them in water to 
keep them fresh. Like human beings, they can exist 
for long periods on water alone, as the liquid forms 
a considerable portion of their bulk. The drinking 
capabilities of plants vary a great deal, and at a Te 
cent scientific exhibition held at Surbiton, England, 
some ingenious devices were shown to register te 
different actions of plant life. The apparatus consists 
of a twin glass tube with a colled formation at the 
base. The flower is Placed through a cork into the 
water in the upper end of the branch tube, and the 
lower end, which is inserted in a vessel filled with 
water, is lifted out and replaced, thus allowing an ait 
bubble to enter the tube. As the plant drinks the we 
ter the bubble is forced along the coiled glass tube a# 
fresh water enters behind it, and the amount taker 
by the flower is, of course, the quantity behind the 
air lobe—From the July Strand.

Danny Meadow Mouse waited until! 
all the rest of Peter Rabbit's friends had 
left the old briar patch after paying 
their respects to Peter and Mrs. Peter. 
He waited for two reasons, did Danny 
Meadow Mouse. In the first place he 
had seen old Granny Fox and Reddy 
Fox hanging about a little way off, and 
though they had disappeared after a 
while Danny had an idea that they 
were not far away, but were hiding so 
that they might catch him on his way 
home. Of course, he hadn't the slight- 
jest intention of giving them the chance. 
He had made up his mind to ask Peter 


if he might spend the night in a corner 
of the old briar patch, and he was very 
sure that Peter would say he might, for 
he and Peter are very good friends, very 
good friends indeed. 

The second reason Danny had for 
waiting was this very friendship. You| 
see, Peter had been away from the Green 
Meadows so long that Danny felt sure 
he couldn't know all about how things 
were there now, and so he wanted to! 
warn Peter that the Green Meadows]! 
were not nearly as safe as before Old 
Man Coyote had come there to live. So 
Danny waited, and when all the rest of | 
the callers nad left he called Peter to 
one side where little Mrs. Peter couldn't | 
hear. Danny stood up on his hind legs 
so as to whisper in one of Peter's ears. 

“Do you’ know that Old Man Coyote 
is the most dangerous enemy we have, 
Peter Rabbit? Do yuo know that?” 

Peter Rabbit shook his head. “I 
don’t believe that, Danny,” said he. “His 
terrible voice has frightened you so that 
you just think him as bad as he sounds. 
“Why, Old Man Coyote is a friend of 
Mine!” Then he told Danny how Old 
Man Coyote had done him a good turn 
in the Old Pasture in return for a 
good turn Peter had once done him, and | 
how he said that he always paid his 
debts.

ful. “What else did he say?” he de- 
manded, 

“Nothin, excepting that we were even 
now,” replied Peter.

LITTLE STORIES FOR BEDTIME 


THORNTON w. FURGESS. 


and he 


him anything, doesn’t owe 
you anything. Watch out, Peter Rab- 
bit! Watch out! I would stick pretty | 


close to the old briar patch with Mrs. | 
Peter if I were you. 1 would indeed, 
You used to think old Granny Fox pret- 
ty smart; but Old Man Coyote is smart- 
er. Yes, sir, he is smarter! And every | 
one of the rest of us has got to be) 
smarter than ever before to keep out of | 
‘is clutches, Watch out, Peter Rab- 
bit, if you and Old Man Coyote are even! | 
Now, if you don't mind, I'll curl up in| 
my old hiding place for the night. I 
really don't dare go back home tonight.” 

Of course Peter told Danny Meadow 
Mouse that he was welcome to spend 
the night in the old briar patch, and 
thanked Danny for his warning as he 
bade him good night. But Peter never 
carries his trouble with him for long, 
and by the time he had rejoined little 
Mrs. Peter he was very much inclined 
to laugh at Danny’s fear. 

‘What did-that funny little Meadow 
Mouse have to say?’ asked Mrs. Peter. 

Peter told her and then added: “But 
I don’t believe we ‘nave anything to 
fear from Old Man Coyote. You know 
he is my friend.” 

“But I don't know that he is mine!” 
replied little Mrs, Peter, and the way 
she said it made Peter look at her anx- 
jously. “I believe Danny Meadow Mouse 
is right,” she continued. “Oh, Peter, you 
will yatch out, won't you?” 

And Peter promised her that he would.

Cox College maintains a thorough 


servatory, Colleze Park, Ga. 


An al fresco party at the historic battlement of Cox College, whose stern 
and warlike features the Virginia creeper, Boston ivy and the beautiful begonia 
vine have transformed into a veritable bower of beauty and recreation. 

The campus of Cox College is well known for its interesting historical feat- 


ures, and the groups of happy girls at their play offer a striking contrast to 
the stern realities of the war of ’65 when si 


tall oaks on the campus where now the girlis! 


Rey. C. W. Bryan, D. D., of China, who lectured to the students last fall, 
_ “Of the 17 colleges I have visited, I consider Cox College with its ideal 
location, beautiful campus, splendid buildings and equipment, to be the best.”

pieces of White Ratine which had just arrived from the “other side.”

In the popular whites which have 


been so scarce this season ...-e+.. 


Of course he wanted to sell.

Because we can sell you $1.25 Ratines at 89c.

they have been in pure white.

Are Advertisements 
Truthful ? 


They are. First, because ninety per cent 
of business men are honorable men. 

Second:—All advertisers have learned 
that it does not pay to fool the public. 

Third:—Newspapers like The Journal 
keep a strict censorship of their advertising 
columns and exclude fraudulent advertise- 
ments. 


; Merchants who advertise are building not 
for today but for the future, They expect 
to be in business for the years to come. 
They know they can only live by service. 
When they advertise a thing they must 
have the goods, and the goods must be ex- 
actly as represented or it would be fatal. 


| There are no merchants in Atlanta so 


deserving of your patronage than those who 
advertise in The Journal. 


The Atlanta Journal

MEN and RELIGION BULLETIN No. 70 


Justice and Mercy in Georgia 


‘SA bruised reed shall he 
not break, 
‘And the smoking flax 


shail he not quench.’’ 
---Isaiah 42; 3. 


<6j will have mercy’’ 
---Matt. 12; 7 


Justice and mercy for the friendless! 


The Senate, without a dissenting vote, has passed the bill proves 
for Probation. 


With only three men voting against it, the Senate has also passed 
the bill providing for the Indeterminate Sentence. 


These two bills are now before the House, having been favorably re- 
ported by the Judiciary Committee. 


And yesterday, Tuesday, CHAIRMAN WHEATLEY saved the 
Girls’ Reformatory from oblivion; he called a special meeting of the 
Committee on Appropriations to consider the Jones-Mills Bill; the RE- 
SULT, it is believed, will be the building of the Reformatory for Girls 
in Georgia. { 


Representatives GRIFFIN, GREENE of Houston, and BERRY, 
members of the sub-committee that considered the original bill and re- 
ported it back carrying an appropriation of $30,000 to the committee 
for action, spoke in favor of it. 


Messrs. CONNOR and METHVIN advocated its adoption. 


JOHN Y. SMITH of Fulton then moved that the Committee report 
favorably the bill as amended. 


In favoring this motion CHAIRMAN WHEATLEY explained that 
he had favored the bill from the beginning. 


Both the Chairman and the members of the Committee recognize 
the APPALLING AND IMMEDIATE NEED for the Reformatory for 
Girls; they unanimously adopted the motion recommending that the bill, 
carrying an appropriation of $30,000, be passed. 


The Prison Commission has urged that the home be built, pointing 
out that Georgia has no place in which to handle wayward and delin- 
quent girls. And the Penitentiary Committee has also, without a dis- 
senting vote, recommended the passage of the Jones-Mills Bill providing 
for the home. 


Remember! This home is not to deal with the hardened woman of 
the street, but with children. 


Today, if a GIRL OF ELEVEN, TWELVE OR FOURTEEN gets 
into trouble, there is no place in Georgia to protect or help her. 


This shame is unendurable. 
years old, known to us, are withoyt a place. 


What will YOU do? : 


No man has opposed—and it is believed that no man can or will 
oppose the bill, either in the House or the Senate. 


Today Georgia looks to the Rules Committee of the House. 


SPEAKER BURWELL, who is ex-officio its chairman, and BLACK- 
BURN of Fulton, its Vice-Chairman, and the men serving with them 
in the Committee on Rules, namely MESSRS. HARDEMAN, MILLER, 
NUNNALLY, PAULK OF BERRIEN, REDWINE, RHODES, SHUP- 
TRINE, SLADE AND RAGLAND, are to decide whether or not the 
State will take care of her delinquent and wayward girls— 


Whether or not the State shall have Probation and the Indetermin- 


ate Sentence— | 


‘Whether or not Georgia will take her stand with her sister states 
in giving justice and mercy to the unfortunate. 


Those who know these men believe that they will NOT KILL, but 
WILL HELP to pass these measures. 


Those who know Georgia believe that the men of the House and 
of the Senate will gladly give the brief time necessary to make these 
bills the law of Georgia. 


They harm no one. But they help the helpless. 


. They mean life to many a home in Georgia where worse than death 
—dishonor and shame, will reign if these bills are not enacted into laws 
at this session of the Legislature. 


What will you do? 


THE EXECUTIVE COMMITTEE OF THE MEN 
AND RELIGION FORWARD MOVEMENT 


Today seventy-five girls under sixteen

THE SPORTLIGHT 
BY GRANTLAND RICE 


THE CLOSING 
The old boy sticks to the job 
And the old boy plays the game; 
He clouts one a mile when the pinch arrives 
Ag he whirls to the mad acclaim; 
But the old boy's wise to the kink, 
He knows where the answer's set 
When he miss one by a foot or two 
That a kid on the bench could get. 


TRAIL. 


Young with the years of life, 
But oid with the driving play, 
He’s out there fighting the answer back 
Through the gray dusk of his day. 
We say he is still a star, 
But he knows where the yarn's complete— 
Where they nail him a foot on a play at first 
That a kid on the bench could beat.

THE CASE OF T. LEACH. 


A good bit has been spoken and written about the wonderful way 
Wagner, Lajoie and others are hanging on, despite their duration or 
service. But in this respect we know of no one who can push throvgh 
an edge by Tommy Leach. Tommy has been reported all in for several 
seasons, and yet today he is still playing fine ball in every department 
with his old speed intact. y crowded him aw: from third where he 
ruled for over twelve y s, and now he is starring at another job on a 
par with almost any in the game. In the salvos of applause that are 
scattered about, Leach deserves enough to rack the stands. 


Jimmy Thorpe is undecided whether he will make the trip around the 
world with Giants and White Sox or whether he will put in the frosty 
autumn season taking back and distributing a number of touchdowns to 
West Point, Penn and Brown. 


what,” writes a bystander, 


They say there is nothing new under the sun, and yet here comes an 
August battle for the cellar championship of the National league that 
doesn’t involve the Boston Brave The Browns emerged last season and 
if the Braves stay out this year, tradition will be ended forever. There'll 
be nothing left to the grand old game but memories.

Ritchie Will 
Fight Welsh 


Articles of Agreement Have 
Not Yet Been Signed by 
Rival Fighters 


SAN FRANCISCO, Aug. 6.—The next 
lightweight match, with a title of the 
ring at stake, will be for the champion- 
ship of the world, Willie Ritchie, the 
American champion, made this a certain- 
ty when he agreed to meet Freddie 
Welsh, champion of Great Britain and 
Australia in Vancouver, B. C., for twen- 
ty rounds September 1. 

Welsh already has one decision over 
Ritchie, awarded in November, 1911, 
when Ritchie substituted at the eleventh 
jour for Ad Wolgast, then the cham- 
pion, who was stricken with appendicitis 
on the eve of a match with Welsh. 
Ritchie had never fought a long dis- 
tance bout then, and went into the match 
untrained. His surprising showing won 
him ‘ais chance at the title against Wol- 
gast. 

Articles stipulating the weight and 
division of the purse will be signed as 
soon as Ritchie returns from a hunting 
trip in the mountains east of Salimas, 
whither he left yesterday afternoon. 

If Ritchie wins at Vancouver, his next 
battle probably will be with Leach Cross 
in New York for ten rounds in the latter 
part of September. 

Except by a knockout, the title could 
not be in peril in a match with Cross, 
and accordingly Ritchie feels fairly con- 
fident that on Thanksgiving day he will 
return to San Francisco for twenty 
rounds either with Harlem Tommy Mur- 
phy or Ad Wolgast. 


77 Miles an Hour 
LEMANS, France, Aug. 6. Bablot, a 
enchman, today won ‘the automobfle 
grand prize of France, covering 335 5-8 


miles in 4 hours, 21 minutes, 50 sec- 
onds. His average speed was 77 miles 
an hour.

Player Reported to White Sox 
When in Lame Condition. 
Chance Was Not Treated 
Nearly So Bad 


BW YORK, Aug. 6.—Having 
Frank Chance's remarks anent the 
of Rollie Zeider, who was handed over 
for Hal Chase and is now in the hos-, 
pital unable to play, Manager Callahan,! 
of the Chicago White Sox, had a reply} 
ready when he arrtyed in town yester-! 
day morning from Boston. In fact, Mr. 
Callahan had many things to say in the 
course of an interview. 
e paid $12,000 cash and gave up 
players valued at $6,500 more for Out- 
fielder Larry Chappelle,” said Callahan, 
“and now we find that Chappelle is un- 
able to play with our team because he 1s 
suffering from an injured groin and a 
twisted knee. In fact, when he came to 
us from the Milwaukee club he was com- 
plaining of his knee, although we had 
been assured that he was able to jump 
right into harness. We are not kicking, 
however, for we know how to take our 
medicine. We are not asking the Mil- 
waukee club to take Chappelle back. 

“I saw what Chance had to say about 
Zeider’s case and I was surprised to 
read that he charged me with bad faith. 
Why, the proposed deal was called off 
before we began to play Zeider on third 
base. Harry Lord was injured on Dec- 
oration day and Zeider finished the 
game. He also played the next two 
when Chance informed me that 
he would accept our terms. 1 told 
Chance that Zeider had played good 
ball for us in two games and part of a 
third and that as far as I knew he was 
fit. If he was spiked by Crawford in 
one of these contests he said nothing to 
me about it. After he joined the New 
York team he played several games be- 
fore he complained of a bunion.

POULTRY. PIGEONS AND LIVE STOCK 


Crop Conditions Through- 
out Georgia 


I MANY sections of the state 


crop conditions at present are 

better than have been known 
for many years, that is for a full 
yield of cotton and corn. Things 
looked very discouraging in the 
early part of the season but splen- 
did rains have come in most sec- 
tions and with plenty of water 
at the right time a bountiful crop 
is sure to follow. In the northern 
and western part of the state, also 
in the eastern part I don’t sup- 
pose there has been a time in 
many years when such splendid 
prospects were so favorable as 
they are at present, and in many 
sections of south Georgia the same 
thing can be said, and the farmers 
of Georgia should feel congratu- 
lated this year that this state of 
affairs exists. They will be in a po- 
sition this fall to diversify their 
farming interests and be prepared 
to do so better than at any time I 
have ever known, and every tarm- 
er who can possibly find his way 
clear to do so should add some 
live stock and poultry to their 
home. I find in a great many 
sections that no new »iooa has 
been added to the thoroughbred 
flocks of poultry, cattle or hogs as 
is usually done by many. For the 
past two years a great many peo- 
ple have neglected keeping their 
stock up.to the standard that they 
should do. This should not be 
neglected longer, and with the 
money in sight this fall everyone 
should try to not only improve 
their poultry but their cattle and 
hogs. A great many breeders are 
now buying good males to use at 
the head of their herds and flocks 
and as soon as these can be secured this summer or fall itshould be done. 
Do not put it off as good specimens are sure to be scarce this coming 
winter and hard to find. 
With plenty feed in 
fimit and continue to 
ever before. The 4 


ight everyone should stock their places to the 

aise feed and live stock and poultry more than 
is coming when the southern farmers wiil depend 
more largely on feeding their corn, grain and also converting their 
rasses and h crops through live stock into cash, and the sooner this 
s done naturally the quicker the fertility of the farms will be built up 
and a more equal distribution of cash throughout the year can be had 
jhan if the cotton crop alone is depended on. The south is sure to be 


a great stock growing country some day, and the ones that get started 
fn it t will, of course, be in a better position to furnish a great many 
breeders with foundation stock than those that wait later. A few 
breeders see thi ate of affairs coming and are preparing themselves 


Yor it, and 1 believe this coming fall and winter will see more good live 


stock and poultry distribution on the farms in the south than has ever 
teen known before. It is only a matter of a short time before thousands 
of dollars will be circulating over the south when the cotton crop 


Yegins to move The fruit and watermelon crops have brought a large 
amount. of money to the growers of southwest Georgia and Florida and 
‘hey are beginning to feel the effects of it, and now right on top of it 
with a good erop of cotton and every {ndication of a good price to be 
paid for it it looks like there is nothing ahead but great prosperity for 
the entire south during the coming fall and winter. 

I have recently made quite an extended trip over the state and it 
is certainly encouraging to see such crops as are NOW beginnings to ma- 
ture in the different parts of Georgia. It means quite a different state 
of affairs from what last fall brought to this country, and the country 


people as well as the city people are sure to be effected by this splendid 
trop that is now in sight throughout this state. During the Farmers 
meeting that is now in progress at Auburn, Ala., there is in attendance 
one thousand or more representative farmers from that state, and most 
of them speak in the most favorable terms of the crop prospects 
throughout Alabama The College of Agriculture at Auburn is con- 


week an auction live stock sale and a large number of 
will be sold at auction at a reasonable price and distrib- 
uted throughout the state of Alabama, This is a good step in the right 
direction and will benefit every section that these animals are carried 
into. Hereafter this will be made an annual feature connected with 
this free summer school and will give many an opportunity to not only 
see but ect the kind of animals that they wish ! 
“There never Was so Many inquiries coming from different parts of 
the south for prices on both live stock and poultry as there is at pres- 
ent. The farmers seem to be thoroughly aroused and now CER they 
are in a position to add these different things to ther fame they sect 
anxious to do so, and are not only asking for prices but are paren ae 
live stock and poultry from different parts of the country. ne reeder 

st few days has bought a splgndia nerd of Jer- 
at an average price of about $500 each.

they have nev been able to do 
before. Yours very truly,

Dawson, Ga., Oct. 26, 1912. 
Aunt Patsy Poultry Feed Co., 
Memphis, Tenn. 
Gentlemen 

Please make us a price on Aunt Patsy’s Poultry Feed, the Egg 
Producer. 

For awhile we were able to buy your feed from our wholesale 
grocer he: but they are out of it about half of the time. I have 
tried to get a supply from them the past week, but they are out and 
insist on selling me other feed which is not worth the bag it is put up in. 

Kindly let me hear from you at once, as my chickens are not laying 
half as they did when 1 had your Aunt Patsy's Feed. 

Respectfully, 
J. H. McLendon. 

Ask Your Dealer for Aunt Patsy Poultry Feed,

are looking for apartments. to 
rent, for rooms for the Win- 
ter and for new locations. 
Don’t handle your renting 
propositions so gingerly that 
you won't be able to find a 
spear of “‘Hay’’ when the 
renting season is over. 


People who want to rent,’ 
or sublet, or buy, are read- 
ing The Journal Want Ads 
daily, and if you are not rep- 
resented with your Journal 
Want Ad*“your best chances 
are lost. 


Now is the time for proper- 
ty owners and agents to make 
“‘hay while the sun shines,” 


and there’s everything to 
gain and there’s nothing to 
lose when you trust your 


renting propositions to The 

Journal Wants. 

Some Want Ads are inserted free 
in The Journal—see first Want Page. 
For charge ads, ask that they be sent 
for, or telephone them to Main 2000 |
